# Spanish — Established Vocabulary/Grammar: Madrigal's Magic Key to Spanish, Part 2 — Alphabetical Cognate Glossary Appendix

**Source:** Margarita Madrigal, *Madrigal's Magic Key to Spanish* (Doubleday hardcover 1951; copyright
1951, 1953, 1989; this trade paperback edition, Broadway Books, 2001), back-of-book alphabetical
Spanish–English glossary appendix, pp. 630–734 (PDF pages 630–734 of 740; clean text layer via
`pdftotext -layout`). This file is a companion to `004_madrigals_magic_key_part2.md`, which covers
the grammar-lesson portion of the same page range (pp. 371–629, including a genuinely high-value
slang/idiom section on pp. 624–629) plus the tail end of the book. It was split out separately per
the extraction spec's "don't let a single file grow unbounded" rule, since this appendix alone runs
to ~2,946 distinct entries across 105 pages.

**Coverage note:** This is genuinely exhaustive — every distinct entry in the book's own alphabetical
glossary (a–z), which the book's own front matter describes as listed "according to the English
alphabet" but which is in practice alphabetized by the Spanish headword (both orderings coincide for
the large cognate majority of entries). It is built around Spanish–English cognates (words with
near-identical spelling/meaning across the two languages, the pedagogical core of Madrigal's "magic
key" method) plus a smaller set of everyday core vocabulary (pronouns, common verbs, connectors) that
rounds the glossary out. The book's own final index (pp. 735–740, a page-locator index of grammar
topics with no new vocabulary or explanatory content of its own) was excluded as out of scope for
this coverage rule — it duplicates section names already captured as Grammar points in
`004_madrigals_magic_key_part2.md` and adds nothing but page numbers.

**Methodology / confidence caveat:** This table was extracted by a small parsing script off the clean
`pdftotext -layout` output (one glossary entry per source line, alphabetically ordered, no column-
scrambling observed on these pages) rather than read and re-typed entry-by-entry, given the entry
count. Term/gloss/gender-marker splitting was verified spot-check-clean across the full alphabetic
range (a–z); one parsing bug (an over-eager "strip stray section-header letter" rule that also
clipped the legitimate one-letter gloss on the entry `yo, I`) was caught and hand-corrected during
review — a sign that the automated approach, while efficient, is not infallible, so treat any
single-row anomaly with mild suspicion rather than assuming perfection. **Part-of-speech tagging is
heuristic** (gender marker `m.`/`f.` present → noun; gloss starts with "to " → verb; `-mente` ending
→ adverb; `-ando`/`-iendo` ending → present participle; a short list of closed-class function words —
pronouns, conjunctions, `no`/`sí`, the preposition `a` — were special-cased) and is **not manually
verified per row**; a handful of irregular/conjugated verb forms and minor function words may carry
an imprecise Part-of-speech value. Gloss text and gender markers were transcribed faithfully from the
source, which is the field this dataset actually depends on. No handwritten marginalia was present —
this source has a genuine, clean text layer end-to-end (`Vision Reading Confidence` = `n/a`
throughout).

---

## Copyright discipline reminder

Selective quotes + paraphrase + analysis only — never bulk reproduction of vocabulary boxes,
dialogue blocks, or explanatory prose. Term/gloss pairs here are short lexical facts (a word and its
one-line translation), not reproduced prose — consistent with how this project treats dictionary/
glossary appendices elsewhere. See `../../00_Reference_Extraction_Spec.md`.
